## 2026-09-17 — Carney Consulting agreement comparison

* **Question:** Whether `CCS- DB  iSmash Seatle agreement (M).pdf` and `Complete_with_Docusign_CCS-_DB__iSmash_Seatl.pdf` are the original contracts.
* **Documents reviewed:** Both are 23-page versions of the Standard Design-Build Agreement for iSmash Seattle WA, between Smash District, LLC and Carney Contracting Services, under the same DocuSign envelope ID `5D2E2F6F-A4E9-49CF-8CBC-272D1EFE7D41`.
* **Comparison:** The extracted agreement text matches on 20 pages and differs only on pages 1, 22, and 23, where the later file adds Divya Mahalingaiah's initials/signature, title `Owner`, and the date `9/9/2025`.
* **Finding:** `CCS- DB  iSmash Seatle agreement (M).pdf` appears to be the later, owner-executed DocuSign export. `Complete_with_Docusign_CCS-_DB__iSmash_Seatl.pdf` appears to be an earlier export that contains Michael Carney's `9/7/2025` execution but not the owner's execution marks.
* **Caveat:** This is a document-content comparison, not legal advice or independent cryptographic validation of the DocuSign audit trail.

## 2026-09-17 — Carney Consulting Everett final agreement

* **Question:** Whether `Construction_Estimate_Increased_iSmash_Everett.pdf` is the final contract with Carney Contracting Services.
* **Finding:** Yes, based on the document itself, it is an executed 52-page Standard Design-Build Agreement and General Conditions for iSmash Everett WA, not only an estimate. It identifies Smash District, LLC as Owner and Carney Contracting Services as Design-Builder.
* **Execution:** The DocuSign envelope is `A8DCE057-B73D-4E01-992E-44B496C24B7F`. The signature page shows Michael A. Carney (January 20, 2026), Divya Mahalingaiah as Owner (January 26, 2026), and Bharath Kumar M N as Coowner (January 26, 2026). The agreement is effective as of the last signature.
* **Commercial amount:** Article 7 states a contract price of `$459,870.00`, subject to Article 8 changes. Exhibit B, Contractor Budget Form, shows the same total build-out construction budget and contains the increased estimate detail.
* **Caveat:** This is a document-content assessment, not legal advice or independent verification of the DocuSign audit trail.

## 2026-09-17 — Everett contract payment schedule

* **Question:** How payment is scheduled under the Everett final agreement.
* **Stated milestones:** (1) at execution/mobilization, 100% of A&E drawings plus 25% of the remaining contract price; (2) 45% of total contract price when rough inspections are scheduled; (3) 20% of total contract price when final inspection is scheduled; and (4) the remaining 10% on Certificate of Occupancy issuance.
* **Invoice workflow:** Carney must submit a Schedule of Values before the first payment application, then submit monthly applications by the 15th after work begins. Owner has seven days to accept/reject, then 30 days after acceptance to pay; the undisputed portion is due within 15 days after a partial rejection. Conditional lien waivers, and for offsite materials bills of sale and insurance, are required as applicable.
* **Owner safeguards:** Retainage is stated as not applicable. Owner may adjust/reject a payment application for enumerated performance, supplier-payment, and defect issues. Final payment requires a conditional final lien waiver and becomes due when the work is fully completed.
* **Material ambiguity to resolve in writing:** Article 9 separately requires 100% of A&E drawings and also states 25%, 45%, 20%, and 10% payment components. Those percentage components already total 100% of the contract price. If the listed A&E line item (`$23,225`) is added to the literal 25%-of-remaining calculation plus the 45%/20%/10% total-contract milestones, the stated draws total approximately `$477,288.75`, exceeding the `$459,870.00` contract price. The agreement needs a signed clarification/amendment before relying on dollar amounts.

## 2026-09-17 — Everett fire-alarm permit delay and final-inspection draw

* **Issue raised:** A fire-alarm permit issue is being handled by the landlord, while Carney has requested the Final Inspection Payment.
* **Permit allocation:** The main agreement says Carney must assist Owner in obtaining building permits and that permit fees are Owner's responsibility, in addition to the contract price. Exhibit B excludes permit and expediting fees. A construction drawing separately says the GC is to secure and pay all permits. The agreement does not specifically identify the fire-alarm permit as landlord-only; this allocation conflict should be resolved against the applicable landlord exhibit, lease, and scope documents.
* **Delay clause:** Section 6.3 grants Carney time extensions for causes beyond its reasonable control, including acts or omissions of Owner or Others and adverse governmental actions, and may allow a price adjustment for qualifying delay costs. The claimed delay needs written notice and documentation under Article 8; the agreement specifies a 10-day notice window, 10-day documentation window, and a Change Order for any contract-price/time change.
* **Payment trigger:** Article 9 defines the Final Inspection Payment as 20% of total contract price upon scheduling the final inspection—not passing it, nor receiving a Certificate of Occupancy. For a `$459,870` price, that is `$91,974`. If final inspection cannot be scheduled because of the permit issue, that milestone is not met on the face of the agreement. If it has been scheduled, Carney should provide the scheduling confirmation, payment application/Schedule of Values support, and required conditional lien waivers. The CO payment is separately the final 10% upon CO issuance.
* **Caveat:** This is a contract-text assessment, not legal advice. The landlord's permit responsibility requires the missing landlord exhibit/lease or other written confirmation.

## 2026-09-17 — Everett 25% opening invoice handling

* **Issue raised:** Carney has submitted an invoice seeking the 25% opening payment.
* **Contract reading:** The first payment is tied to execution/mobilization, not to final inspection or Certificate of Occupancy. The fire-alarm permit delay therefore does not, by itself, negate the opening-draw trigger. The agreement nevertheless requires a Schedule of Values before the first payment application and conditional lien waivers for parties seeking payment.
* **Amount ambiguity:** The contract says the first draw is 25% of the remaining contract price, plus 100% of A&E drawings. It does not cleanly define the A&E-drawings amount, and Exhibit B both lists `$23,225` for architectural/engineering services with permitting and separately excludes A&E drawings. Do not assume an invoice stated as 25% of the full `$459,870` price (`$114,967.50`) matches the contract language; 25% of the balance after the `$23,225` line would be `$109,161.25`, before any separately agreed A&E amount.
* **Recommended response path:** Respond in writing within the agreement's seven-day review window; request a payment application in the Exhibit C form, Schedule of Values, calculation/reconciliation to the contract price, conditional lien waivers, and written clarification of the A&E and milestone math. Accept only an undisputed amount, if any, and state the specific basis for any partial rejection. Under the agreement, the undisputed portion of a partially rejected application is payable within 15 days after initial partial rejection; accepted amounts are payable within 30 days after acceptance.
* **Caveat:** No opinion was provided on the actual invoice because it was not attached. This is a contract-text review, not legal advice.

## 2026-09-17 — Everett percentage-draw dollar breakdown

* **Contract amount:** `$459,870.00`.
* **Simple four-milestone math (which totals the contract price):** 25% execution/mobilization = `$114,967.50`; 45% rough-inspection scheduling = `$206,941.50`; 20% final-inspection scheduling = `$91,974.00`; 10% Certificate of Occupancy = `$45,987.00`; total = `$459,870.00`.
* **Literal first-draw wording creates a different result:** If Exhibit B's `$23,225.00` architectural/engineering line is the separate 100% A&E amount, the remaining price is `$436,645.00`, and 25% of it is `$109,161.25`. The first draw would then be `$132,386.25` when A&E is included; adding the later 45%/20%/10% draws produces `$477,288.75`, or `$17,418.75` above contract price.
* **Conclusion:** The agreement needs a written, signed clarification that either treats the 25%/45%/20%/10% sequence as the complete payment schedule or specifies a different base for later percentages and the precise A&E payment amount.

## 2026-09-17 — iSmash franchise-requirements obligation review

* **Question:** Whether Carney is obligated to comply with iSmash franchise requirements and complete the project accordingly.
* **Finding:** The agreement identifies the project as iSmash Everett but does not contain an express promise that Carney will comply with iSmash franchise requirements, obtain franchise approval, or deliver a franchise-approved completed location.
* **What is binding:** The agreement requires construction in accordance with the owner-approved Construction Documents. Article 14 incorporates the most current owner-approved design documents and gives written amendments/Change Orders highest precedence. Therefore, an iSmash standard that is actually embodied in those approved documents, or later added by signed amendment/Change Order, can define contractual requirements.
* **Drawing reference:** A life-safety drawing says contractors must comply with the landlord's Exhibit C and reference an `FHS Design Manual` before bidding. `FHS` is not identified as iSmash in the agreement, and the stated reference is pre-bid rather than a clear completion/acceptance condition. It should not be treated as proof of an iSmash franchise obligation without the referenced document and a written incorporation.
* **Recommended contractual fix:** Obtain the current iSmash franchise/design requirements with version/date, define any scope gaps, and incorporate them by a signed amendment or Change Order that specifies acceptance criteria, responsible party, price/time impact, and treatment of existing work.
* **Caveat:** This is a contract-text assessment, not legal advice.

## 2026-09-17 — Loading Dock Gate Allowance search

* **Question:** Whether the executed Everett agreement mentions `Loading Dock Gate Allowance`.
* **Finding:** No. A full-text search of the 52-page agreement and incorporated exhibits found no mention of the exact phrase or the close combinations `loading dock`, `dock`, or `gate` as a construction allowance. The sole `allowance` hit concerns making allowance in HVAC pricing for duct/piping routing around obstructions, which is unrelated.
* **Implication:** The reviewed agreement does not identify a loading-dock-gate allowance, scope item, amount, or payment trigger. If it appears in a proposal, estimate, landlord document, invoice, or change-order request, that separate document is needed to assess whether it is contractual.

## 2026-09-17 — Latest Carney email on disputed change order and payment

* **Source:** Email thread from Troy Fink, Carney Contracting Services, dated September 17, 2026, 12:13 PM–12:35 PM, supplied by Bharath Mysore.
* **Carney's stated position:** At 12:24 PM, Troy wrote, “We are halting all work till this is resolved.” At 12:35 PM, responding to a request for updated plans, he wrote: “Not until we are sent our last progress payment. Will send today.” The last sentence appears to mean Carney will send the last-progress-payment request today and will not provide updated plans until it is paid; the wording is ambiguous.
* **Disputed CO items identified by Bharath:** Structural steel iSmash rooms (`B2512-031`) `$9,175.00`; HVAC permit (`M2603-036`) `$4,428.92`; plumbing permit (`P2603-044`) `$2,214.44`; electrical permit (`E2603-056`) `$2,450.00`; wing-wall demolition `$999.60`; steel-plate drilling `$8,700.00`. Total: `$27,967.96`.
* **User's stated dispute basis:** Wing-wall demolition was not necessary. Steel-plate drilling was reportedly agreed at Carney's May 29 meeting (read.ai) to be at no charge. The permit/compliance items were sent to the City for verification.
* **Contract-process note:** The executed Everett agreement requires a Change Order for work/price/time changes and sets a written notice process. Its stop-work provision applies when a payment is due and unpaid after written notice that work will stop within seven days; the supplied same-day email states an immediate halt and does not itself show that earlier contractual notice or that the invoice amount is due. Earlier notice or additional documents could change this assessment.

## 2026-09-17 — Historical correspondence and evidence-source capture

* **Scope:** Read-only evidence capture for the Carney/Onesta LLC contractor group, iSmash corporate, Quaint Oak Bank, and Alex Jo/NW Prime. The search window began January 1, 2024.
* **Correspondence coverage:** A date-filtered mailbox scan examined 2,572 messages from 2024–2025 and identified 779 messages matching the specified organizations or Alex's address. The earliest identified message is dated January 13, 2025; no matching 2024 message was found. The separate 2026 correspondence capture remains 961 unique messages.
* **Attachment evidence:** Attachment metadata was cataloged for 95 matching 2025 messages (290 attachments) and 100 matching 2026 messages (371 attachments), totaling 661 attachment records. The register includes source-message date, subject, sender, Outlook message/attachment identifiers, file name, MIME type, size, inline status, and an Outlook source link.
* **Local source repository:** A read-only inventory captured 379 files under `/Users/bmysoren/Desktop/2026/iSmash/`, including 325 PDFs, 23 Word documents, 9 spreadsheets, CAD/ZIP files, and images. No document content was copied, edited, or deleted.
* **Created evidence references:** `outlook_historical_correspondence_inventory.csv`; `outlook_attachment_evidence_inventory.csv`; and `local_franchise_source_inventory.csv`. Together with the prior 2026 correspondence inventory, these are source manifests—not legal conclusions or authenticity determinations.
* **Search caveat:** Historical messages were identified from relevant header fields, subject lines, or the displayed message preview. A thread may match because it contains quoted earlier correspondence. The mailbox and the original local files remain the authoritative content sources.

## 2026-09-17 — Contractor-role correction

* **Actual General Contractor:** Carney Contracting Services / `carneycontracting.com`.
* **Local GC / local contractor:** Onesta LLC / `onestallc.com`, acting locally in connection with the Carney-led project.
* **Use in future records:** References to “the GC” mean Carney unless the context expressly identifies Onesta LLC as the local GC or subcontractor.

## 2026-09-17 — Recommended response to Troy Fink’s latest payment/work-stoppage email

* **Verified latest message:** Troy Fink’s 12:35 PM `Re: CO` message states, “Not until we are sent our last progress payment. Will send today,” in response to the request for updated plans. His 12:24 PM message states, “We are halting all work till this is resolved.”
* **Immediate position:** Do not authorize a new payment or change order merely because of those emails. The phrase “last progress payment” does not identify an invoice, milestone, amount, Schedule-of-Values support, accepted payment application, or contractual basis.
* **Requested documentation:** Ask Carney to identify the exact invoice and milestone; provide the payment application, Schedule of Values, calculation against the contract price, applicable conditional lien waivers, proof of the asserted milestone trigger, and any written contractual stop-work notice on which it relies. Separately request the executable/current plan files and the City-resubmittal status.
* **Contract review point:** On the agreement reviewed, the 20% draw is tied to scheduling final inspection and the final 10% draw to issuance of a Certificate of Occupancy. The agreement also specifies a payment-application review process and a written seven-day stop-work notice process for due and unpaid amounts. The supplied same-day messages do not themselves establish those prerequisites; earlier correspondence and the actual invoice may affect the position.
* **Action sequence:** Confirm the bank’s stop/cancellation status in writing; send a controlled written partial-rejection/request-for-information response; preserve the emails and payment records; obtain written final-inspection/fire-alarm status from the City and landlord-side permit team; and consult Washington construction counsel promptly before an extended work stoppage or any payment decision.

## 2026-09-17 — Washington construction-counsel referral sources

* **Recommended matter description for intake:** Commercial owner-side design-build/construction dispute in Everett involving a disputed change order, claimed progress payment, threatened/actual work stoppage, permits/inspection delay, potential lien exposure, and connected franchise, landlord, and SBA-loan issues.
* **Sources identified:** Snohomish County Bar Association Lawyer Consultation Referral; Washington State Bar Association Legal Directory; and the WSBA Construction Law Section’s attorney/community resources.
* **Referral-process note:** The Snohomish County service states that consultations may take weeks and are not for emergencies. Given the active work stoppage, direct outreach to Puget Sound lawyers who handle commercial construction disputes may be faster.
* **Conflict check:** Before sharing detailed records, request a conflicts check against Carney Contracting Services, Onesta LLC, iSmash/Smash Franchising, NW Prime/Alex Jo, Quaint Oak Bank, and the named individuals.

## 2026-09-17 — Non-court escalation for Carney nonresponse

* **Issue reported:** Troy Fink and Ron Tobiasz are not responding to messages or calls. The owner wants to complete the project without going to court.
* **Contract path:** Article 12 provides a non-court sequence: direct discussions by authorized representatives; written escalation to senior executives if unresolved after five business days; mediation under the AAA Construction Industry Mediation Rules if direct discussions fail; then binding arbitration only if mediation does not resolve the dispute. The agreement expressly waives ordinary court/jury resolution for covered contract disputes.
* **Work-continuance point:** Article 12.1 says that, unless agreed otherwise in writing, Carney must continue work and maintain the approved schedules during dispute mitigation/resolution, while the owner continues payments in accordance with the agreement. This should be raised in a controlled written notice, not as an unsupported accusation.
* **Recommended action:** Send a single Article 12.2 direct-discussions notice to Troy, Ron, and Carney senior management. Record September 17, 2026 as the first discussion date; state that the owner does not agree to work suspension; offer two meeting times within the next two business days; request an authorized Carney representative; and request the underlying payment/plan documents. If no agreement is reached after five business days, issue the contract’s written senior-executive escalation and request mediation rather than filing court litigation.
* **Payment balance:** Do not approve a disputed change order merely to restart work. At the same time, promptly review any properly supported payment application and handle a demonstrably undisputed amount under the agreement, because Article 12.1 requires payments in accordance with the agreement and Article 12.7 preserves lien rights.

## 2026-09-17 — Plain-language explanation of Article 12 (Dispute Resolution)

* **Purpose:** Article 12 does not determine whether Carney's invoice or change order is valid. It establishes the required non-court sequence for resolving an unresolved contract dispute.
* **12.1 — Work and payment continue:** Unless the parties agree otherwise in writing, Carney must continue the work and maintain the approved schedule while the dispute is being addressed. The owner must continue making payments that are due under the agreement. It does not require payment merely because Carney requests it; the contractual payment requirements still apply.
* **12.2 — Direct discussion, then executives:** Representatives with authority should try to resolve the issue and document the first-discussion date. If unresolved after five business days, they must notify their senior executives in writing. Senior executives then have five business days to meet. If still unresolved 15 days after the first discussion, the parties move to the next dispute-resolution stage.
* **12.3 — Mediation:** The parties attempt mediation under the AAA Construction Industry Mediation Rules or mutually selected rules. It must be convened within 30 business days of the first discussion and conclude within 45 business days; costs are shared equally. Either side can end the mediation after its first session by written notice.
* **12.4–12.5 — Binding arbitration:** If mediation fails, the contract calls for binding AAA construction arbitration rather than an ordinary court/jury trial. AAA Fast Track Rules apply if neither side's disclosed claim or counterclaim exceeds $250,000.
* **12.6–12.7 — Other parties and liens:** The parties should include other necessary parties in one process where possible, but this does not automatically bind iSmash or Onesta LLC without applicable agreements. The article does not waive Carney's statutory lien rights.
* **Relationship to change orders:** Article 8 controls the claim/change-order process. Article 12 is the path when that process or a payment/work dispute remains unresolved.

## 2026-09-17 — Plain-language explanation of Article 8 (Changes in the Work)

* **Purpose:** Article 8 governs changes outside the signed scope: additional/different work, price increases, time extensions, concealed conditions, and certain escalation claims. It is the process relevant to Carney's disputed change-order request.
* **8.1 — Formal Change Order required:** A scope, price, or completion-date change must be authorized by an applicable Change Order. Carney may request one and the owner may order one, but the parties must negotiate in good faith. A change-price adjustment must separately identify design-service cost. Carney is not obligated to perform changed work until the Change Order is executed.
* **8.2 — Minor no-cost/no-time changes:** Carney can make genuinely minor, contract-consistent changes only if they do not affect price, schedule, or material quality/performance. It must promptly notify the owner in writing and record the change in its documents.
* **8.3 — Concealed/unknown conditions:** Carney must stop only the affected work, promptly give written notice, and allow the owner to investigate. The owner issues an interim directive. Work related to that unknown condition needs written mutual agreement; resulting price/time issues follow Article 8.
* **8.4 — Escalation:** Significant material, labor, or equipment price increases or shortages/delays can justify an equitable price/time request, but this is still subject to the Article 8 claim process.
* **8.5 — Price and disputed-scope work:** Price changes are to be a mutually accepted, itemized lump sum. If the parties dispute whether requested work is already within the original scope, Carney must give an estimate using the owner's scope interpretation. If the owner then issues a written order to proceed, the owner pays 50% of that estimate while both sides preserve their final position. This 50% mechanism is not automatic; it is triggered by the owner's written proceed order.
* **8.6 — Notice deadlines:** For a price/time claim, Carney must give written notice within 10 days after the event or recognizing the condition, normally before starting the work; then supporting documentation within 10 more days. The owner must approve or deny in writing within five days after receiving that documentation; no response is deemed a denial. Any resulting price/time change still requires a Change Order.
* **Application to the current CO:** Ask Carney to identify the event, notice date, supporting documents, work date, itemized calculation, and signed Change Order for each line item. A later invoice or email demand does not by itself show compliance with Article 8 or prove the item is outside base scope.
* **Terminology warning:** “CO” means Change Order in Article 8. The Article 9 “CO Payment” means Certificate of Occupancy payment; it is not a change-order payment.

## 2026-09-17 — Site-condition / ADA-ramp notice analysis

* **Contract mechanism:** Article 8.3 applies if Carney encounters a physical site condition materially different from the Contract Documents or an unusual unknown condition not ordinarily inherent in the work. Carney must stop only the affected work after observing it, promptly give written notice to the owner, allow the owner to investigate, and await an owner interim directive. Work relating to the condition requires written mutual agreement; price/time changes are then determined under Article 8.
* **Separate claim deadline:** Under Article 8.6, any Carney claim for added price or time must be noticed in writing within 10 days after the triggering event or when Carney first recognizes it, normally before proceeding, with supporting documents due within a further 10 days. Any price/time adjustment still requires a Change Order.
* **ADA-ramp application:** An existing noncompliant ADA ramp may be a qualifying site condition only if it was genuinely unknown or materially different from the approved documents. If the ramp/egress condition was visible, depicted, or known to Carney or Onesta LLC earlier, Article 8.3 may not fit. Carney's failure to provide a written notice when it or Onesta LLC first recognized the issue materially weakens a later Article 8 price/time claim, but does not automatically determine all legal rights.
* **Design-document point:** Article 3.1.2 requires Construction Documents to be based on applicable codes/laws at preparation and requires Carney to identify in writing material changes/deviations when submitting them. If Carney's approved documents addressed this ramp/egress route, that is separate evidence relevant to responsibility.
* **Evidence/request:** Request the date and source of Carney's and Onesta LLC's first knowledge; all Article 8.3 and 8.6 notices; original and revised drawings; City/inspector communications; affected-work stop records; cost/time backup; and any signed Change Order. Preserve the March/April communications or meeting recordings showing that Onesta LLC flagged the condition.

## 2026-09-17 — Article 8 verification and practical meaning

* **Verified source:** Executed Everett agreement, Article 8, PDF pages 12-13 (contract sections 8.1-8.6).
* **Meaning:** Scope, price, or time changes require a Change Order; minor no-price/no-time changes require prompt written notice; concealed/unknown site conditions require Carney to stop affected work, promptly notify the owner in writing, and await an owner interim directive and mutual written agreement. Escalation claims also use this process.
* **Disputed scope:** A price change is a mutually accepted itemized lump sum. For scope disagreements, Carney must provide an estimate under the owner's interpretation. Only if the owner gives a written order to proceed does the contract require a 50% interim payment of that estimate while preserving each party's position.
* **Claim timing:** Carney must give written notice of price/time claims within 10 days after the event/recognition (normally before work), provide supporting documentation within another 10 days, and the owner has five days to approve or deny; silence is a denial. A resulting price/time adjustment still needs a Change Order.

## 2026-09-18 — Lease allocation: base building, code compliance, fire alarm, and sprinklers

* **Express fire-alarm promise — Landlord:** Exhibit D, “Landlord’s Work,” states that NW Prime “agrees to install a fire alarm system if one is requested and required by the City of Everett or other governmental authority having jurisdiction.” The owner’s April 10 and April 14 emails to Alex document notice/request and describe the City requirement; the City requirement itself should remain supported by the City/Fire Marshal record.
* **Tenant-specific fire protection — Tenant:** Exhibit E requires Tenant, at its expense, to provide fire-alarm, fire-sprinkler, and other work **in addition to Landlord’s Work** needed to complete the Premises under the approved Tenant plans. Section 6.9 identifies fire alarm, monitoring, fire prevention/safety equipment, and emergency equipment or lighting additional to the base system supplied by Landlord. This makes additions or TI-driven work beyond the base system a Tenant responsibility.
* **Sprinkler layout changes:** Exhibit E Section 6.8 says modifications/relocation of sprinklers to suit Tenant requirements are performed by Landlord’s sprinkler contractor at Tenant expense. Exhibit E also requires Landlord's building engineer, sprinkler contractor, and fire-alarm contractor to be involved before work on those systems, and requires the alarm to be disarmed/rearmed. Those coordination provisions do not themselves decide cost.
* **General building / City compliance is divided:** Tenant bears broad obligations for governmental requirements tied to the Premises, Tenant operations, occupancy and Tenant's Work (Articles 6.7 and 8.5; Exhibit E). Tenant must obtain Tenant-work approvals and the CO. Landlord must maintain the Project and Common Areas and has responsibility for foundation, structural elements, roof, exterior walls, systems not exclusively serving the Premises, and latent building/Premises/mechanical-system defects (Articles 7.4 and 9.1). The lease does not contain a blanket promise that Landlord will bring every existing building condition into code compliance.
* **Application to the current dispute:** If the City-required fire alarm is the base system contemplated by Exhibit D, the lease supports asking NW Prime to install it. If the required work is an additional system, alteration, relocation, or capacity change caused by the iSmash TI/use, the Tenant-side provisions become relevant. Similarly, an existing shared/exterior egress condition may fall more naturally under Project/Common Area or non-exclusive building systems, but the lease alone does not conclusively classify the specific stair/ramp condition. Preserve the City’s written classification and the approved scope.

## 2026-09-18 — Whether to ask Carney to include stair revisions to expedite opening

* **Evidence reviewed:** The preserved September 14–17 email thread, September 18 Read.ai transcript, and saved September 18 landlord/City correspondence summaries. No new Outlook refresh was performed for this advice.
* **Conflicting coordination positions:** In Alex's September 15 email (preserved thread, lines 95–97), Alex relayed his architect's position that the exit modifications should be incorporated in the TI plans and that a separate submission would restart approval. That is reported advice, not direct written City confirmation. On September 17, Bharath agreed to obtain approved drawings/permit, with NW Prime supplying labor (lines 7–9). The later September 18 call discussed landlord responsibility for the exterior steps, and Scott said the landlord was submitting the stair plans (Read.ai 8:17; lines 124–125); Bharath's later email excluded exterior stair work from TI plans and asked NW Prime to coordinate it.
* **Submission status caveat:** Ron initially said he would submit his stamped plan Monday (Read.ai 8:03; lines 118–119), but Troy later stated Carney would not move forward until agreement on all change orders (33:03; lines 569–570). The Monday filing therefore needs fresh written confirmation; it is not established as an unconditional commitment.
* **Recommendation:** Contact Ron and Troy, copying Alex and iSmash, for one coordinated decision. Ask the design professional and City plan reviewer to confirm whether including the second-door stair/guard details in B2512-031 or a separate linked landlord permit is the accepted and faster route. Obtain concrete drawing, filing, exterior-work, and inspection dates. Consider inclusion if it shortens the overall path to CO; do not assume either inclusion or exclusion automatically reduces review time. Confirm the party responsible for exterior construction separately from who prepares/submits the drawings.
* **Drafting constraints:** Keep the coordination email focused on the selected second-door route, submission, responsibility, and timeline. Honor the owner's preference to omit payment and plan-change cost discussion. Do not treat this advice as an authorization to change scope or send an email.
* **General City reference:** Everett's public permitting guidance explains that required documents depend on permit type/scope and that incomplete submissions or extensive corrections can lengthen review. It does not establish the correct permit split for this project: https://www.everettwa.gov/2686/All-documents.

He stated that City-required corrections should remain in the tenant permit/TI scope unless the City confirms in writing that a specific correction is an independent, building-wide structural issue unrelated to the tenant improvements, use, or occupancy. * **Lease-language review note:** The available lease materials define Tenant's Work in Exhibit E as work other than Landlord's Work required to complete the Premises and open for the permitted use, and require that Tenant's Work comply with governmental requirements. Exhibit D separately identifies Landlord's Work, including specified base HVAC, an ADA-compliant restroom, and installation of a fire alarm system if City of Everett requires one. The available draft's Section 6.7 is a mechanical/HVAC-distribution provision, not a general permit/occupancy provision. Alex's statement is therefore a broad lease interpretation; the classification of an existing exterior stair/guard as Tenant's Work versus a separate building condition remains the operative unresolved factual/legal question.

## 2026-09-21 — High-low drinking fountain: Contract-document review

* **Approved design documents incorporated into the Carney agreement:** Article 14.1(b), PDF page 22, makes the most-current Owner-approved design documents Contract Documents. In `Construction_Estimate_Increased_iSmash_Everett.pdf`, the precise drawing citations are: PDF page 25, sheet `G-01.0`, Cover Sheet/Building Data, listing “1 - HIGH/LOW DRINKING FOUNTAIN” as provided; PDF page 33, sheet `P-0.1`, Plumbing Notes, Symbols, Abbreviations & Specifications, defining “PROVIDE” as “To furnish and install”; PDF page 35, sheet `P-1.0`, Water Floor Plan, Water Key Note 3, directing that the existing drinking fountain be replaced with a new drinking fountain and requiring field verification/replacement of existing piping if needed; and PDF page 36, sheet `P-1.1`, Sanitary Floor Plan, Sanitary and Vent Key Note 4, with the same replacement/piping direction. These are the contractual drawing references. This review did not substantiate the earlier separate statement about a demolition-sheet direction or an electrical receptacle callout.
* **Budget qualification:** Exhibit B does not separately price a high/low drinking fountain; it lists “General Plumbing (Internal Distribution)” as ETR and contains a general exclusion for “Owner Furnished items.” It does not specifically identify the high/low unit as owner-furnished. Accordingly, the design documents support that the unit is part of the required Work, but the budget alone does not conclusively resolve who supplies the fixture. Under Article 14, the Agreement governs over approved design documents if there is a direct conflict. No executed change order or specific owner-furnished schedule was identified in this review.
* **Related email evidence:** On September 1, 2026, Codie Morgan (Onesta) emailed Ron Tobiasz with photos of the “filter needed for the water fountain,” copied to Bharath. This reflects a field discussion about an existing fountain/filter; it does not by itself establish the contractual responsibility for furnishing the required new high/low unit.

## 2026-09-23 — Recommended response position: disputed Change Orders

* **Purpose:** Preserve the distinction between permit-fee Change Orders that Bharath has approved and other Change Orders whose scope, City basis, price, or prior agreement remains disputed. Do not sign a disputed Change Order solely to obtain a work update.
* **Payment clarification:** Because Carney has stated that the $15,000 receipt is not yet allocated, request in writing that it remain unapplied pending a written allocation agreement. State that the transfer and prior payment discussions are not acceptance of any disputed Change Order, modification of the contract sum, waiver, release, or authorization of disputed work. Do not send an additional payment or repeat a total figure until Carney produces a revised invoice that identifies each signed Change Order, any credit, and the exact balance. Prior messages contain inconsistent disputed-CO totals.
* **Requested documentation:** Ask Carney to identify: (1) the specific signed Change Orders it regards as approved; (2) its proposed allocation of the $15,000; (3) the revised invoice and underlying City receipts for pass-through permit amounts; (4) each disputed CO, its scope and supporting records; and (5) the Agreement clause it believes permits withholding project status, plan revisions, or schedule information pending signature of disputed COs.
* **Legal-context note:** Washington's private-construction change-order statute addresses issuance of a change order for the dollar amount of additional work that is not in dispute after satisfactory completion. It does not itself resolve the parties' contract dispute or determine whether Carney may suspend work; the signed Agreement and a Washington construction lawyer's advice control those questions.

## 2026-09-23 — Effect of signing a Change Order under the Everett Agreement

* **No blanket-release finding:** The reviewed Everett Agreement does not state that signing a Change Order makes every owner dispute, claim, or right “void,” nor does Article 8 contain a general release of unrelated claims.
* **Practical effect of an executed CO:** Article 8.1 treats an executed Change Order as the formal authorization for the scope, contract-price, and/or completion-date change it describes. Therefore, a signature may be strong evidence of agreement to the particular item, price, and time impact contained in that CO even though it is not a blanket release.
* **Express preservation route:** Article 8.5 contemplates a disputed-scope situation. If Carney estimates the work using the Owner's scope interpretation and the Owner then issues a written order to proceed, the agreement calls for a 50% payment of that estimate while the parties preserve their final positions. This is not automatic and should not be confused with signing a standard disputed CO; use it only with written clarification of scope, payment allocation, schedule, and preservation of rights.

## 2026-09-23 — Contract distinction: disputed changed work versus stoppage of all work

* **Article 8:** Carney is not obligated to perform genuinely changed work until the applicable Change Order is executed. That provision can support a refusal to perform the particular added/change-order scope while price or scope is unresolved.
* **Article 12.1:** Unless the parties agree otherwise in writing, the Agreement states that work and the approved schedule continue while a dispute is addressed, and the Owner continues paying amounts that are due under the Agreement. It is not a requirement to pay a disputed invoice merely because it was demanded.
* **Resulting issue:** A refusal to perform only new, disputed CO work may be consistent with Article 8. A refusal to perform all original/base-contract work, withhold ordinary project information, or halt the whole project requires a separate contractual basis, such as the Agreement's payment-default/stop-work provision and its notice requirements. The existing emails state Carney's condition but do not identify the clause, distinguish base work from disputed added work, or establish compliance with the required notice process.
* **Needed written clarification:** Ask Carney to categorize each stopped item as base-contract work, changed work, or work stopped for alleged nonpayment; identify the governing provision, the due/undisputed invoice balance, and any contractual notice it relies on. This is a contract-text assessment, not a legal conclusion that Carney has breached.

## 2026-09-25 — Drinking-fountain compliance research (current code)

* **Current governing code:** Everett states that the 2024 Washington codes will not take effect until May 3, 2027; the project is therefore within the current 2021-code environment. Under 2021 Washington Building Code Section 2902.5.1, an occupant load over 30 requires one drinking fountain for the first 150 occupants. The approved iSmash cover sheet states an occupant load of 72.
* **Bottle-filling-station limitation:** Section 2902.5.4.2 permits bottle-filling stations to substitute for up to 50% of required fountains only where more than two drinking fountains are required per floor or secured area. On this plan, a stand-alone bottle-filling station therefore does not appear to replace the single required fountain. A combination unit that has an accessible wheelchair-height drinking spout, a standing-person-height spout, and a bottle filler may satisfy the fixture/accessibility requirements without a separate additional fountain, subject to City confirmation.
* **Possible compliant paths to confirm in writing with Everett:** (1) document that the existing station is a compliant combination high/low drinking fountain plus bottle filler; (2) use an existing public drinking fountain on an accessible route, if within the code's 300-foot maximum travel distance for a covered/open mall (or 500 feet outside that context); or (3) obtain an approved revised plan identifying a compliant replacement or alternate design. Do not remove or omit the planned fixture based only on an informal discussion; obtain the plan-reviewer/inspector's written determination tied to permit B2512-031.

These records support an absence of produced written City substantiation and Carney's direction that Bharath verify the asserted field requirement; they do not constitute a Troy admission that Carney had no vendor receipts or invoices.

Later emails also conditioned plan-revision submission and further progress on CO signature/payment.
* **Contract comparison:** Section 8.1.3 permits Carney to decline genuinely changed work until a Change Order is executed. Section 9.1.3 permits stopping the Work for an amount actually due only after written notice stating that work will stop within seven days and expiration of that seven-day period. Section 12.1 separately requires Carney to continue the Work and approved schedule during dispute resolution while the Owner continues payments properly due under the Agreement. The record therefore needs to distinguish disputed extra work from original/base-scope work and determine whether Carney issued a compliant prior notice for a due, undisputed amount.
* **Legal/evidentiary significance:** Subject to counsel's review, the record may support theories involving breach, waiver/modification or apparent authority, equitable estoppel, improper billing, failure to substantiate a claimed Change Order, and economic duress/coercive payment context. It does not automatically invalidate the signed/paid CO, prove fraud, establish legal duress, or guarantee reimbursement. Bharath's contemporaneous written reservation when signing/paying is important and should be paired with the stoppage evidence.
* **Authentication and consent safeguards:** Read.ai text and speaker labels are machine-generated derivatives. Preserve the original audio/video, recording notice or participant-consent evidence, meeting invite and participant list, platform metadata, original transcript export, hashes, and an unedited copy of every email/text. Confirm disputed quotations against the audio. Washington generally requires all-party consent for recording a private conversation; counsel should confirm that the meeting's Read.ai notice/participation satisfied that requirement before using the recording or transcript in a claim.
* **Documents still needed:** Obtain the executed and reissued drilling COs with DocuSign audit certificates; all drilling quotes, invoices, receipts, proof of payment, delivery/return records and labor logs; all requests for backup and Carney responses; the original meeting recording and consent notice; every stop-work/default notice; the relevant payment applications and allocation ledger; and evidence identifying which base-scope tasks or plan duties stopped and for how long.

## 2026-09-29 — User clarification: Carney retained Onesta LLC as local GC

* **Contracting structure stated by Bharath:** Smash District, LLC contracted directly with Carney Contracting Services as Design-Builder. Bharath states that Carney, in turn, hired Onesta LLC as the local general contractor/site contractor for the Everett work. Onesta was not separately hired by Smash District under the reviewed owner-Carney agreement.
* **Contract allocation:** Section 5.2 of the owner-Carney agreement makes Carney responsible for management of its subcontractors in performance of their work, while Section 4.4.3 states that the Owner has no contractual obligations to subcontractors, suppliers, or the Design Professional. Accordingly, Onesta's local role does not, by itself, transfer Carney's contractual responsibilities to Onesta or make Smash District responsible for Carney's payments to Onesta.
* **Washington-registration issue:** Onesta's registration, bond, insurance, and permit role must be verified independently. Its registration would not automatically satisfy or replace any Washington registration required of Carney if Carney itself undertook, offered, supervised, or consulted on the construction as the contracting Design-Builder/general contractor. Obtain the exact legal names, UBI numbers, contractor-registration numbers, registration histories, bonds, insurance, and the Carney-Onesta subcontract.
* **Lien/payment safeguard:** Because Onesta and lower-tier parties may assert statutory lien rights if unpaid, require Carney to provide conditional and final lien waivers from Onesta and material subcontractors/suppliers with applicable payments. Do not pay Onesta directly or alter payment allocation without a written agreement addressing credit against the Carney contract, lien releases, authority, and lender requirements.

## 2026-09-30 — User clarification: alleged changed work preceded the Change Orders

* **User-reported sequence:** Bharath states that Carney/its local subcontractors performed the work later described in the disputed Change Orders before Carney issued the Change Orders and before Bharath approved or executed them. The later COs therefore sought retroactive approval and payment rather than prospective authorization. The exact work dates, Carney's first-knowledge dates, CO issue dates, and any alleged oral authorization still must be established item by item.
* **Contract requirements:** Section 2.4.3 defines a Change Order as a written order signed by both Owner and Design-Builder. Section 8.1.1 requires changes in the Work to be authorized by an applicable Change Order; §8.1.3 says Carney is not obligated to perform changed work until a CO has been executed; §8.5.1 requires a mutually accepted, itemized lump sum; and §8.6 requires written claim notice within ten days and, except in an emergency, before proceeding, followed within ten days by appropriate supporting documentation. If Carney relies on a concealed or unknown site condition, §8.3 additionally required it to stop the affected work, promptly notify Bharath, allow investigation, obtain an Owner interim directive, and reach written mutual agreement before related work.
* **Preliminary contractual effect:** If the reported sequence is confirmed and no emergency, prior written Owner directive, or unequivocal waiver applies, Carney did not follow Article 8's notice, documentation, authorization, and pricing procedure. That is a substantial defense to Carney's entitlement to increase the Contract Price and supports a contract-breach/unauthorized-billing theory. It does not by itself establish that the physical work was unnecessary, that Carney incurred no cost, or that fraud occurred.
* **Carney's likely responses:** Carney may contend that Bharath orally requested or authorized the work, knew it would cost extra, previously accepted informal/post-work CO practice, or later ratified the charges by signing and paying the COs. Mere awareness that work was occurring is different from informed authorization of a Contract Price increase. Bharath's contemporaneous objections, verification demands, payment-under-protest language, and §13.7 no-waiver clause weigh against an unequivocal-waiver theory, but the signed CO text and precise reservation remain important.
* **Evidence needed by line item:** Build a chronology showing the alleged directive/condition date, who knew what, work start and completion dates, daily logs and photos, first written notice, first price disclosure, CO transmission and signature timestamps, invoice/payment date, and Bharath's contemporaneous response. Require Carney to identify any emergency or written Owner directive on which it relies and to produce the Article 8.6 notice and supporting documentation.
* **Fraud distinction:** Retroactive CO issuance is contractual noncompliance, not automatically fraud. A fraud theory becomes stronger only if Carney knowingly misstated an existing fact—such as prior Owner approval, a City directive, an incurred/paid vendor amount, or actual permit fees—to induce signature or payment. Bharath's prior disbelief and objection may create a reliance issue for common-law fraud, making breach, declaratory/refund relief, duress, or a properly supported Consumer Protection Act theory potentially better fits.

## 2026-09-30 — Claimed March/April compliance-notice delay and loss causation

* **User's position:** Bharath states that, had Carney promptly communicated the City compliance issue when the City allegedly raised it with Carney's representative in March or April 2026, the design, landlord coordination, permit revision, corrective construction, and inspection work could have been completed by now. Bharath attributes the resulting opening delay and losses to Carney's nondisclosure.
* **Verified April record and important qualification:** On April 6, Bharath asked Ron for the exact wording of the dock and ADA-compliance changes so he could begin the landlord discussion, and he followed up from April 7–10 for details and photographs. Bharath also told landlord Alex on April 6 that the contractor had reported an issue with the emergency-exit ramp and that Bharath was seeking the inspector's written details. Ron's located replies addressed loose/dry-rotted dock wood and photographs but did not provide a substantive ramp finding, code citation, correction design, urgency, or schedule impact. The presently supportable theory is therefore failure to investigate, provide **actionable specifics**, and coordinate a timely correction—not that Bharath or Alex had no general notice of a possible ramp issue.
* **Causation evidence and factual distinction:** In the May 29 project call, Ron described the project as approximately one week from closeout once the fire-alarm work cleared and discussed calling final inspections without identifying the ramp as a critical-path blocker. In the August 28 call, he expressed confidence about egress based on the fire inspection. The actual occupancy-blocking determination was documented around September 1–4, when the City treated the ramp/second-exit condition as preventing TCO. This supports a failure-to-investigate/schedule theory, but Carney may argue that the April ramp concern and September stair/alternate-exit correction were different issues and that a fire inspection did not decide building or accessibility compliance.
* **Potential contract breach:** If the alleged City communication and timing are authenticated, the facts may support breach of Carney's duties under §3.2.3 to give required notices and comply with applicable law, §3.2.4 to maintain and revise a schedule identifying required Owner information or approvals, §3.2.7 to provide written progress reporting, and §5.2 to manage its subcontractors. If the compliance issue required changed work, Article 8's prompt written notice, investigation, authorization, and Change Order procedures are also relevant. Carney remains the Owner's contracting Design-Builder even if the City communication first reached Onesta, GPN, or another Carney-retained participant acting within its project role.
* **What must be proven:** Liability is not established merely by saying the project would have finished earlier. The claim needs proof of (1) the exact City correction or compliance direction; (2) when, how, and to whom it was communicated; (3) Carney's contractual duty and failure to relay or act on it; (4) a reliable counterfactual critical-path schedule showing the work probably would have completed earlier with timely notice; (5) separation of delay caused by the fire-alarm permit, landlord work, City review, Owner decisions, material lead times, or other concurrent causes; (6) reasonable mitigation; and (7) actual, documented damages tied to the avoidable delay period.
* **Potentially recoverable direct losses:** Subject to counsel's review, stronger categories include duplicate or avoidable architecture/engineering work, resubmittal and reinspection charges, corrective or rework costs, reasonable acceleration/expediting costs, added completion-contractor costs, and return of unsupported or unauthorized charges—but only to the extent the evidence connects each cost to Carney's late communication rather than to the underlying correction itself.
* **Major contractual damages restriction:** Section 3.2.3 makes Carney liable for compliance failures only for loss, cost, and expense “not otherwise waived.” Section 6.4 then expressly waives consequential, indirect, special, and punitive damages, including loss of use, rental expense, loss of income or profit, financing-related losses, loss of business, office overhead, reputation, and insolvency. Those provisions give Carney a substantial defense to the largest opening-delay categories—rent/carrying cost, lost store revenue or profit, financing cost, and business interruption—even if breach and delay causation are proven. Section 6.5 also caps Carney's aggregate contractual liability at 50% of the Contract Price; using the original `$459,870` Contract Price gives an illustrative cap of `$229,935`, but counsel must determine the adjusted Contract Price and which remedies fall within the cap.
* **Possible but uncertain challenges to the waiver/cap:** Deliberate concealment, fraudulent inducement or misrepresentation, a properly supported Consumer Protection Act claim, willful misconduct, an independent tort duty, or restitution/return of an overpayment may create arguments that a particular remedy is outside or unenforceable under the waiver or cap. These are fact- and clause-specific arguments, not automatic exceptions. Merely relabeling a contract breach as gross negligence generally does not create an independent tort claim for purely economic loss.
* **Critical evidence package:** Obtain the City's original dated correction record and delivery metadata; identify every Carney/Onesta/GPN recipient; preserve all March–April emails, texts, meeting recordings, daily logs, BuilderTrend entries, schedules, and the contrasting fire-alarm notice chain; document the date Bharath first received the actual requirement; build an actual-versus-counterfactual critical-path timeline; and maintain a damages ledger with invoices, payment proof, dates, mitigation steps, and allocation of concurrent delay.
* **Preservation/procedure:** Do not wait until the store opens to preserve the claim. A written notice should identify the first known City communication, the late-disclosure theory, affected critical-path activities, direct costs incurred to date, continuing damages subject to the contract, and an express reservation of all rights. Follow Article 12's direct-discussion, mediation, and binding arbitration procedure. Avoid accusing Carney of fraud in project correspondence until the source records and counsel support that allegation.

## 2026-09-30 — Carney noncommunication as evidence of contractor-caused delay

* **User-reported continuing pattern:** Bharath states that Carney is not responding reliably by email, telephone, or text. This can support a breach and delay theory when Carney-controlled information, a drawing, City/inspector coordination, a subcontractor direction, a schedule update, or another Carney action was required before a critical task could advance. Silence is strongest evidence when a dated request identified the needed action, Carney alone controlled it, a promised or reasonable response date passed, and the dependent work remained blocked.
* **Stronger than silence alone:** The current record includes Troy's September 17 written statement that Carney was halting all work, September 18 recorded statements that Carney would not submit plans or take another step until the Change Orders were resolved, and September 21–23 communications conditioning progress and schedule information on signed and paid Change Orders. Those affirmative stoppage statements are stronger proof than an unanswered call or email, subject to determining whether Carney had a contractual right to withhold a particular changed-work item and whether it complied with any required notice.
* **Contract duties implicated:** Sections 3.2.4 and 3.2.7 require Carney to maintain/revise the work schedule and provide periodic written progress reports; §5.2 makes Carney responsible for managing subcontractors; §6.3.3 requires both sides to mitigate delays; and §12.1 requires Carney to continue the Work and maintain approved schedules during dispute resolution while the Owner continues payments required by the Agreement. Section 8.1.3 may permit Carney to decline genuinely changed work before execution of a Change Order, but it does not necessarily authorize a halt of undisputed base-scope work or withholding of basic schedule information.
* **Burden and causation:** Noncommunication does not automatically prove every delayed day, and Carney does not initially have to prove a universal negative. Smash District should first establish, by dated evidence, the duty, unanswered request or omitted disclosure, precise task that could not proceed, avoidable critical-path days, mitigation, and resulting direct loss. Carney can then rebut the chronology with contemporaneous schedules, daily logs, written delay/time-extension notices, City and landlord dependencies, permit transmittals, subcontractor records, proof of completed available work, and a day-by-day showing that the same opening date would have resulted even with prompt responses.
* **Likely concurrent-delay defenses:** Carney is likely to rely on the fire-alarm process through August 28, City review time, the landlord-controlled exterior stair and permit, owner-controlled fountain/loading-gate/setup work, disputed payment/Change Order issues, and the September 24 delivery of revised drawings. Each period should therefore be allocated separately rather than alleging that Carney caused all project delay: (1) March/April–August 28, lost opportunity to investigate and perform egress work concurrently; (2) September 1–17, egress as an occupancy blocker and delayed design/coordination; (3) September 17–24, the strongest explicit Carney stoppage window; and (4) September 24 onward, separate Carney plan filing/base-scope closeout from landlord, City, Owner, and franchise dependencies.
* **Evidence matrix:** For each unanswered contact, preserve the date/time/method, sender and recipient, exact requested deliverable, contract duty or Carney promise, follow-ups, task waiting on the answer, earliest achievable completion with a timely response, actual response/completion, net critical-path days, and direct cost. Preserve call logs, voicemails, texts, emails with headers, BuilderTrend exports, schedules, City records, and Carney-Onesta/GPN communications. A missing-record or credibility argument can strengthen an otherwise proven case, but missing records do not substitute for proof of an essential causation fact.
* **Recommended claim framing:** The strongest statement is not merely “Carney did not communicate.” It is: “Carney failed to provide specified contract-required information and coordination, and in identified periods expressly withheld plans/work, causing the following critical-path activities to start or finish later.” Serve a formal, counsel-reviewed notice of continuing delay and reservation of rights under the contract rather than relying only on routine follow-ups; request Carney's complete project schedule, updates, delay notices, daily reports, BuilderTrend history, RFIs, permit/design transmittals, subcontractor logs, and all records supporting each claimed owner/City/landlord-caused delay. The consequential-damages waiver, liability cap, Article 12 procedure, and arbitration clause remain material constraints.

## 2026-10-04 — Planned post-occupancy Carney claim and recovery expectations

* **User's present intention:** Bharath plans to pursue Carney for alleged delay, failure to honor the agreement, disputed/unsupported Change Orders and related losses after the City of Everett issues the Certificate of Occupancy.
* **Procedure qualification:** The reviewed agreement appears to require direct discussions, senior-executive escalation, mediation and then binding AAA construction arbitration for covered disputes, rather than an ordinary court/jury lawsuit. Washington's Uniform Arbitration Act generally treats a written arbitration agreement as enforceable and permits a court to compel arbitration. Counsel must determine the exact forum and whether any asserted statutory or independent claim falls outside the clause.
* **Do not defer preservation:** Waiting for the Certificate of Occupancy may help establish the final delay period and damages cutoff, but Bharath should not wait to preserve evidence, give any contractually required notice, notify applicable insurers, or obtain counsel. Section 9.4.2 reportedly creates claim-waiver risk at final payment; no final payment, lien release, settlement release or waiver should be signed without counsel reviewing the reservation of rights.
* **Current strongest recovery theory:** refund/credit for unsupported or unauthorized charges and documented direct costs caused by Carney, such as duplicate design, resubmittal/reinspection, corrective/rework, acceleration or completion-contractor expense. Each item requires a dated invoice/payment record, duty, breach, causation, mitigation and separation from concurrent City, landlord, fire-alarm, owner or material delays.
* **Major contractual barrier:** Section 6.4 reportedly waives consequential, indirect, special and punitive damages, including lost use, rent, income/profit, financing losses, business interruption, overhead and reputation. Section 6.5 reportedly caps aggregate contractual liability, including costs/expenses/attorney fees, at 50% of the Contract Price. On the original `$459,870` price the illustrative cap is `$229,935`; counsel must verify the executed text, adjusted Contract Price, enforceability and which remedies or claims fall inside or outside the cap.
* **Seven-figure expectation:** The present record does not support a reliable expectation of a multimillion-dollar recovery. Such an outcome would require millions in legally recoverable and well-proven damages, successful avoidance or invalidation of the waiver and cap through a viable independent theory, causation despite concurrent delays, mitigation proof, and a collectible defendant or insurer. Retroactive COs, missing receipts or poor communication alone do not establish fraud or defeat the contract limitations.

## 2026-09-30 — Contractual application of Washington law to Carney

* **Express governing-law clause:** Article 13.4 states, “The Law in effect at the location of the Project shall govern this Agreement.” Article 1 identifies the Project as “iSmash Everett WA (Seattle WA),” and §2.4.28 defines the Worksite by the Project location in Article 1. The contract therefore selects Washington substantive law to govern the agreement because the project is located in Everett, Washington.
* **Compliance obligation:** Section 2.4.13 defines “Law” as applicable federal, state, or local law, ordinance, code, rule, or regulation. Section 3.2.3 requires Carney, as Design-Builder, to give required notices and comply with all such Laws in effect as of the agreement date at its own cost, with liability for unwaived loss, cost, or expense attributable to its failure. This includes applicable Washington law and Everett/local requirements; it does not mean that every Washington statute applies regardless of subject matter.
* **Out-of-state contractor/local-GC distinction:** Carney's location outside Washington and its retention of Onesta as local GC do not displace Article 13.4 or Carney's direct contractual duties. Separately, Washington's contractor-registration statute broadly includes entities undertaking, bidding, superintending, or consulting on construction, and generally requires every contractor to register. Whether Carney's exact activities and registration history satisfy chapter 18.27 RCW should be verified with L&I; Onesta's registration would not automatically answer Carney's own status.
* **Forum distinction:** Governing law is not the same as venue. The reviewed provisions do not specify a Washington county or arbitration seat. Article 12 requires direct discussions, mediation, and then binding AAA construction arbitration for claims arising from the agreement, with judgment enforceable in a court having jurisdiction. Subject to counsel's review, an arbitrator should apply Washington substantive law, while AAA rules and any later locale determination govern arbitration procedure.

## 2026-10-14 — High-low fountain: specific no-additional-cost statements and qualifications

* **Question:** Whether Carney said all plan work is financially covered, and specifically whether the high-low fountain shown in the plans is included within the agreed price. Reviewed the preserved transcripts, the signed revised contract/plan PDF, and eight focused Outlook search pages. This was not a full mailbox refresh, and no outgoing email or payment action occurred.
* **Strongest direct Carney statement — October 2, 2026:** In [the raw transcript](evidence/read_ai/raw_exports/2026-10-02-fw-everett-weekly-calls__owner-supplied.txt), Ronald Tobiasz at 4:40 (lines 105–106) says, “Then we will definitely handle that 100%” after the discussion identifies the fountain replacement on the drawings. At 5:00 (lines 111–112), discussing ordering the high-low unit and scheduling it, he says, “you could quote me on this: if it's on the drawings, there will be no cost to Bharath.” Preserve the drawing condition. This is a specific fountain-cost commitment in a machine-generated transcript, not an unlimited promise that every plan item is included regardless of exclusions or subsequent changes.
* **Further corroboration — October 9:** In [the raw transcript](evidence/read_ai/raw_exports/2026-10-09-everett-weekly-calls.txt), Troy at 4:00 and Ron at 4:11 expressly list the high-low fountain among the remaining work (lines 53–57). Troy at 4:39 asks whether there are no change orders for any of that work; Ron at 4:52 answers “None of that” (lines 61–65), then separately discusses a possible exit-sign electrical charge. The fountain is not identified as that exception. The later 5:22–5:43 discussion concerns plan-correction charges: Ron says the team absorbed them, but Troy still wants confirmation with Mike. Do not treat that separate item as an unconditional fee waiver or mix it into the fountain exchange.
* **Plan support independently rechecked:** In `/Users/bmysoren/Desktop/2026/iSmash/Construction/Construction_Estimate_Increased_iSmash_Everett.pdf`, PDF page 22, §14.1(b), incorporates the most current Owner-approved design documents as of the Commencement date. PDF page 25, G-01.0, lists one high/low drinking fountain as required and provided and carries Carney/owner initials. PDF page 29, A-00.1, reads “DEMO EXISTING IN-WALL WATER FOUNTAIN. PREPARE FOR NEW HI-LO UNIT.” This now substantiates the demolition-sheet reference that the September 21 review did not substantiate. PDF page 33, P-0.1, requires materials/labor for complete operable plumbing as shown and defines “PROVIDE” as “TO FURNISH AND INSTALL.” PDF pages 35–36, P-1.0 note 3 and P-1.1 note 4, direct replacement of the existing drinking fountain and verification/replacement of associated piping if required.
* **Contract-price qualification:** PDF page 11, Article 7, states $459,870 subject to Article 8 adjustment. PDF page 24, Exhibit B, has no separately priced fountain line; general plumbing is ETR. It contains broad exclusions including Owner Furnished items and ADA Upgrades, and a qualification excluding service upgrades/replacements. The fountain is not specifically named as excluded or owner-furnished. Article 14.2 gives Change Orders/written amendments, then the Agreement, precedence over design documents. Accordingly the plans strongly show required work, but the contract/budget alone should not be described as conclusively resolving every supply/payment issue. The later specific no-cost/no-change-order statements are materially stronger support for the fountain position. Inclusion in the agreed total is not proof the entire contract balance has already been paid.
* **Previously unverified owner email now located:** The October 2 transcript says Ron had an email requesting a bottle filler. A direct sent email was located: September 21, 2026 at 6:26:30 PM PDT, subject “Re: 25-124 Everette WA changes,” Bharath wrote, “Ron, In the plan we are using High-low water fountain. This should only be a Bottle filling station.” The October 2 and October 9 commitments came after that email. Preserve it as a real qualification to the history; do not say the owner never requested a different fixture. The email alone does not establish a signed scope deletion or agreed price change.
* **No blanket coverage finding:** Broader language located in April 11, 2025 (Michael Carney) concerns the separately priced drawing package, not all construction. April 17's broad build-out statement is Kevin Macmaster's (franchisor) and contains exclusions. April 2, 2026 Ron's “whole build included” is specifically the cash wrap. Additionally, Troy's February 9, 2026 email claims the polished-concrete finish shown on plans was not included because the proposal retained existing flooring; that is Carney's position on a different item, not a fountain ruling. Do not extrapolate a universal all-plans/all-costs guarantee from these records.
* **Preservation and practical wording:** Saved the four selected email records and search audit in [the focused fountain evidence record](evidence/emails/2026-10-14_fountain_scope_search.json), including the direct owner email and the contrary flooring example. Original transcripts and PDFs were not edited. The evidence-supported position is that Carney specifically stated the high-low fountain shown in the drawings would be handled without additional cost/change order, subject to confirming the applicable drawing and any subsequent agreed amendment. Check important transcript wording and speaker attribution against recording when available; no conclusion about legal enforceability or completed installation is made here.

## 2026-10-18 — Signed lease review: existing building code noncompliance

* **Question and source:** Bharath asked whether the lease addresses a build not approved by the City, then clarified that he means an existing building that is not compliant with City codes. Reviewed relevant pages visually in `/Users/bmysoren/Desktop/2026/iSmash/Lease-2025-SmashDistrictLLC.pdf` (58-page scanned PDF). PDF page 2 bears an August 4, 2025 reference date; PDF page 31 contains landlord and tenant signatures. Page references below are physical PDF pages, not printed page numbers. This is a document-based reading, not a determination of liability or available legal remedies; later signed amendments have not been reconciled in this review.
* **Section 9.1, PDF page 14 (printed 13):** Landlord's maintenance responsibility includes the foundation, structural elements, roof, exterior walls and systems not exclusively serving the Premises. Separately and expressly, Landlord at its sole cost and expense is responsible for repairing all latent defects in the Building, Premises and/or mechanical systems during the term and extensions. Tenant has substantial nonstructural/exclusive-system maintenance duties. Do not extend the express sole-cost language for latent defects to every common-area expense, or assume that every pre-existing or visible code issue is a latent defect.
* **Section 1.6, PDF page 3 (printed 2):** The as-is/where-is/all-faults provision is expressly subject to latent defects, with HVAC, electrical systems and ADA-compliant restrooms in good working condition and repair. It also disclaims general fitness/suitability warranties and places approved-use/fire-protection verification duties on Tenant. It is not a blanket warranty that the entire Building complies with every City code.
* **Sections 7.4 and 5.1.2, PDF pages 12 and 6–7:** Landlord maintains the Project/Common Areas excluding the Premises in good, clean and operating condition, with reimbursement to the extent allowed by Article 5. The specific “Building Defects” operating-cost exclusion on PDF page 7 includes correction of design/construction defects. Other Article 5 provisions permit some governmental-compliance capital expenses, so neither common-area maintenance nor compliance costs should be characterized categorically as non-pass-through without reading the applicable exceptions.
* **Tenant-side counterweights:** Main lease Section 6.7, PDF page 11, assigns Tenant municipal requirements occasioned by or required in conducting Tenant's business. Section 8.5, PDF page 13, requires Tenant at its expense to comply with governmental requirements applicable to the Premises or its operations. Exhibit E Section 2, PDF pages 44–45, assigns Tenant permits, occupancy approvals and upgrades required to conduct its business. Exhibit D, PDF page 43, expressly includes certain landlord HVAC, ADA-restroom and City-required fire-alarm work, but otherwise broadly disclaims landlord assistance with Tenant's Work/repairs/improvements needed to obtain permits and open, subject to latent defects. These provisions must be considered alongside Section 9.1, not omitted.
* **Numbering clarification:** Main lease Section 6.7 is “Compliance with Laws.” Exhibit E has its own Section 6.7 concerning HVAC distribution. Earlier notes that treated the exhibit's numbering as the main lease's Section 6.7 must not be relied on for that identification.
* **Application to the exterior ramp/stairs:** The landlord-responsibility argument is stronger if the correction concerns a latent building defect, assigned structural responsibility or common-area maintenance, rather than a tenant-use/occupancy-triggered upgrade. Existing/exterior location alone does not conclusively determine contractual responsibility, and a City correction required for Tenant's CO does not by itself settle the private cost allocation. Useful evidence is the City's written correction/code citation plus a qualified designer's explanation of the condition and why the work is required. No finding was made that the ramp/stair condition is legally a latent defect, that either party breached, or that rent may be withheld.
* **Separate initial permit contingency:** Rider 1, PDF page 32, provides a termination right if Tenant cannot obtain the Building Permit for construction of its business within 90 days of Delivery of the Premises, with deposit/prepaid-amount refund if terminated under that contingency. This is not an identified blanket continuing right to terminate or abate rent for a later inspection/CO denial. No contingency deadline, current rent commencement date or surviving termination right was calculated.
* **Preservation:** This entry supplements the existing record; no earlier messages, evidence, lease files or other documents were deleted or changed. No notice, payment instruction or third-party communication was sent.
